I don't have any routines or anything I feel like I just have to do over and over. I mean, I fidget a lot, like I said, but it's not a habit or a ritual I have to complete. It's just me not being able to sit still. So, no, nothing like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking a lot more than others in social situations?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I can hold a conversation, sure, but I'm not the one dominating the room or talking over everybody else. I don't really feel like I talk more than anyone else does.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Very easily. Like, when I'm trying to sort my bills or go through paperwork at the kitchen table... I just can't stay on it. If I hear a car door close outside, or if my cell phone lights up, I look right away. Sometimes even just a thought about the garden will pop into my head, and that's it, I'm completely pulled off track and the bills just sit there.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. My mind does jump around a lot from one thing to the next, but I wouldn't say I get stuck on the same repeating thoughts that just won't go away. It's more of a constant buzzing of different things rather than one thing playing on a loop.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes, all the time. I don't really feel like I talk more than anyone else does.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Very easily. Like, when I'm trying to sort my bills or go through paperwork at the kitchen table... I just can't stay on it. If I hear a car door close outside, or if my cell phone lights up, I look right away. Sometimes even just a thought about the garden will pop into my head, and that's it, I'm completely pulled off track and the bills just sit there.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I interrupt people a lot more than I mean to, honestly. I notice it especially when I'm in my committee meetings. I end up doing it even when someone else is still right in the middle of talking.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your restlessness and inability to stay still are causing problems for you at work or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, it really is causing problems. It's... well, it's gotten to the point where my family has had to step in and take over dealing with my bills and paperwork because I just can't sit still long enough to get through them. And it's affecting my relationships, too. People at my church and the garden club... they've had to redo plans or just cover for responsibilities I've dropped because I get so restless and distracted. I just feel so embarrassed about it. These are groups I really care about, and now they feel like they can't count on me anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking excessively or dominating conversations?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think I do that. I mean, I interrupt people, like I said, because the thoughts just pop out... but I don't think I talk too much overall or try to take over the whole conversation. I end up doing it even when someone else is still right in the middle of talking.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your restlessness and inability to stay still are causing problems for you at work or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, it really is causing problems. It's... well, it's gotten to the point where my family has had to step in and take over dealing with my bills and paperwork because I just can't sit still long enough to get through them. And it's affecting my relationships, too. People at my church and the garden club... they've had to redo plans or just cover for responsibilities I've dropped because I get so restless and distracted. I just feel so embarrassed about it. These are groups I really care about, and now they feel like they can't count on me anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking excessively or dominating conversations?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think I do that. I mean, I interrupt people, like I said, because the thoughts just pop out... but I don't think I talk too much overall or try to take over the whole conversation. It's more just blurting things out at the wrong time rather than going on and on.\n\nDoctor: Were you very physically active and restless as a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I was. Even back in grade school, I was always on the move. I remember my teachers would write on my report cards that I was a bright girl, but careless. I was always losing my papers, talking out of turn, and they constantly had to give me reminders to just stay in my seat or actually finish the work in front of me. So, yes, the restlessness has been there for a very long time.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. It's like I constantly have to jump up and do the next thing. Even when I'm completely worn out and I know I should just be sitting still, I just can't seem to do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that keep repeating in your mind and won't go away?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really have anything like that. My mind does jump around a lot from one thing to the next, but I wouldn't say I get stuck on the same repeating thoughts that just won't go away. It's more of a constant buzzing of different things rather than one thing playing on a loop.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes, all the time. It's more just blurting things out at the wrong time rather than going on and on.\n\nDoctor: Were you very physically active and restless as a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I was. Even back in grade school, I was always on the move. I remember my teachers would write on my report cards that I was a bright girl, but careless. So, yes, the restlessness has been there for a very long time.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these feelings of restlessness and difficulty staying still consistently throughout your adult life?\n\nPatient: Well, like I said, I was always an active kid, and I guess I've always been someone who likes to keep busy.     "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, doctor. Well, I came in today because... I just feel like I cannot settle inside my own skin anymore. It's like I constantly have to jump up and do the next thing. Even when I'm completely worn out and I know I should just be sitting still, I just can't seem to do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that keep repeating in your mind and won't go away?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really have anything like that. My mind does jump around a lot from one thing to the next, but I wouldn't say I get stuck on the same repeating thoughts that just won't go away. It's more of a constant buzzing of different things rather than one thing playing on a loop.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes, all the time. It's... well, even in church meetings, I just can't seem to keep still. I'll be tapping my foot, or just rubbing the edge of my purse over and over. And I'm always shifting around in the chair. It gets to the point where the ladies beside me actually start looking over at me because of all the moving. I just can't seem to stop doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't think I have a problem with that. I mean, I know I'm always moving around and fidgeting, but I don't just... jump into things without thinking them through first. I usually know what I'm doing before I do it. So, no, I wouldn't say I act on impulse.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any habits or rituals that you feel compelled to perform over and over?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I don't have any routines or anything I feel like I just have to do over and over. I mean, I fidget a lot, like I said, but it's not a habit or a ritual I have to complete. It's just me not being able to sit still. So, no, nothing like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking a lot more than others in social situations?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I can hold a conversation, sure, but I'm not the one dominating the room or talking over everybody else. I don't really feel like I talk more than anyone else does.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Very easily. Like, when I'm trying to sort my bills or go through paperwork at the kitchen table... I just can't stay on it. If I hear a car door close outside, or if my cell phone lights up, I look right away. Sometimes even just a thought about the garden will pop into my head, and that's it, I'm completely pulled off track and the bills just sit there.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I interrupt people a lot more than I mean to, honestly. I notice it especially when I'm in my committee meetings. A thought will just pop into my head, and... well, I just blurt it out right then and there before I lose it. I end up doing it even when someone else is still right in the middle of talking.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your restlessness and inability to stay still are causing problems for you at work or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, it really is causing problems. It's... well, it's gotten to the point where my family has had to step in and take over dealing with my bills and paperwork because I just can't sit still long enough to get through them. And it's affecting my relationships, too. People at my church and the garden club... they've had to redo plans or just cover for responsibilities I've dropped because I get so restless and distracted. I just feel so embarrassed about it. These are groups I really care about, and now they feel like they can't count on me anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking excessively or dominating conversations?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think I do that. I mean, I interrupt people, like I said, because the thoughts just pop out... but I don't think I talk too much overall or try to take over the whole conversation. It's more just blurting things out at the wrong time rather than going on and on.\n\nDoctor: Were you very physically active and restless as a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I was. Even back in grade school, I was always on the move. I remember my teachers would write on my report cards that I was a bright girl, but careless. I was always losing my papers, talking out of turn, and they constantly had to give me reminders to just stay in my seat or actually finish the work in front of me. So, yes, the restlessness has been there for a very long time.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these feelings of restlessness and difficulty staying still consistently throughout your adult life?\n\nPatient: Well, like I said, I was always an active kid, and I guess I've always been someone who likes to keep busy. But... no, it hasn't been this bad my whole adult life. It's really only been causing real trouble since my husband died. That was about a year and ten months ago. Before that, things were manageable, but since he passed... that's when the restlessness just got so much harder to handle.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant symptoms of inattention, such as difficulty sustaining focus during tasks, for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. It's been going on for a while now, well over six months. I'll sit down to read something simple, like the newsletter from the senior center or the schedule for my choir, and I start out fine. But then, after just a few minutes, I realize my eyes are just moving over the same lines again and again. I get to the end of a page and have absolutely no idea what I just read. It's very frustrating.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, doctor. Well, I came in today because... I just feel like I cannot settle inside my own skin anymore. It's like I constantly have to jump up and do the next thing. Even when I'm completely worn out and I know I should just be sitting still, I just can't seem to do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that keep repeating in your mind and won't go away?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really have anything like that. My mind does jump around a lot from one thing to the next, but I wouldn't say I get stuck on the same repeating thoughts that just won't go away. It's more of a constant buzzing of different things rather than one thing playing on a loop.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes, all the time. It's... well, even in church meetings, I just can't seem to keep still. I'll be tapping my foot, or just rubbing the edge of my purse over and over. And I'm always shifting around in the chair. It gets to the point where the ladies beside me actually start looking over at me because of all the moving. I just can't seem to stop doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't think I have a problem with that. I mean, I know I'm always moving around and fidgeting, but I don't just... jump into things without thinking them through first. I usually know what I'm doing before I do it. So, no, I wouldn't say I act on impulse.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any habits or rituals that you feel compelled to perform over and over?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I don't have any routines or anything I feel like I just have to do over and over. I mean, I fidget a lot, like I said, but it's not a habit or a ritual I have to complete. It's just me not being able to sit still. So, no, nothing like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking a lot more than others in social situations?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I can hold a conversation, sure, but I'm not the one dominating the room or talking over everybody else. I don't really feel like I talk more than anyone else does.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Very easily. Like, when I'm trying to sort my bills or go through paperwork at the kitchen table... I just can't stay on it. If I hear a car door close outside, or if my cell phone lights up, I look right away. Sometimes even just a thought about the garden will pop into my head, and that's it, I'm completely pulled off track and the bills just sit there.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I interrupt people a lot more than I mean to, honestly. I notice it especially when I'm in my committee meetings. A thought will just pop into my head, and... well, I just blurt it out right then and there before I lose it. I end up doing it even when someone else is still right in the middle of talking.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your restlessness and inability to stay still are causing problems for you at work or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, it really is causing problems. It's... well, it's gotten to the point where my family has had to step in and take over dealing with my bills and paperwork because I just can't sit still long enough to get through them. And it's affecting my relationships, too. People at my church and the garden club... they've had to redo plans or just cover for responsibilities I've dropped because I get so restless and distracted. I just feel so embarrassed about it. These are groups I really care about, and now they feel like they can't count on me anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking excessively or dominating conversations?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think I do that. I mean, I interrupt people, like I said, because the thoughts just pop out... but I don't think I talk too much overall or try to take over the whole conversation. It's more just blurting things out at the wrong time rather than going on and on.\n\nDoctor: Were you very physically active and restless as a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I was. Even back in grade school, I was always on the move. I remember my teachers would write on my report cards that I was a bright girl, but careless. I was always losing my papers, talking out of turn, and they constantly had to give me reminders to just stay in my seat or actually finish the work in front of me. So, yes, the restlessness has been there for a very long time.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these feelings of restlessness and difficulty staying still consistently throughout your adult life?\n\nPatient: Well, like I said, I was always an active kid, and I guess I've always been someone who likes to keep busy. But... no, it hasn't been this bad my whole adult life. It's really only been causing real trouble since my husband died. That was about a year and ten months ago. Before that, things were manageable, but since he passed... that's when the restlessness just got so much harder to handle.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant symptoms of inattention, such as difficulty sustaining focus during tasks, for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. It's been going on for a while now, well over six months. I'll sit down to read something simple, like the newsletter from the senior center or the schedule for my choir, and I start out fine. But then, after just a few minutes, I realize my eyes are just moving over the same lines again and again. I get to the end of a page and have absolutely no idea what I just read. It's very frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Were you often described as hyperactive or having too much energy when you were a child?\n\nPatient: I think I pretty much covered that a minute ago. I don't really remember if people used the exact word 'hyperactive' back then, but like I said, I was always on the move and my teachers were constantly telling me to sit still. So I guess they thought I had too much energy, but I don't really have anything else to add to that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.   {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, doctor. Well, I came in today because... I just feel like I cannot settle inside my own skin anymore. It's like I constantly have to jump up and do the next thing. Even when I'm completely worn out and I know I should just be sitting still, I just can't seem to do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that keep repeating in your mind and won't go away?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really have anything like that. My mind does jump around a lot from one thing to the next, but I wouldn't say I get stuck on the same repeating thoughts that just won't go away. It's more of a constant buzzing of different things rather than one thing playing on a loop.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes, all the time. It's... well, even in church meetings, I just can't seem to keep still. I'll be tapping my foot, or just rubbing the edge of my purse over and over. And I'm always shifting around in the chair. It gets to the point where the ladies beside me actually start looking over at me because of all the moving. I just can't seem to stop doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't think I have a problem with that. I mean, I know I'm always moving around and fidgeting, but I don't just... jump into things without thinking them through first. I usually know what I'm doing before I do it. So, no, I wouldn't say I act on impulse.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any habits or rituals that you feel compelled to perform over and over?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I don't have any routines or anything I feel like I just have to do over and over. I mean, I fidget a lot, like I said, but it's not a habit or a ritual I have to complete. It's just me not being able to sit still. So, no, nothing like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking a lot more than others in social situations?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I can hold a conversation, sure, but I'm not the one dominating the room or talking over everybody else. I don't really feel like I talk more than anyone else does.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Very easily. Like, when I'm trying to sort my bills or go through paperwork at the kitchen table... I just can't stay on it. If I hear a car door close outside, or if my cell phone lights up, I look right away. Sometimes even just a thought about the garden will pop into my head, and that's it, I'm completely pulled off track and the bills just sit there.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I interrupt people a lot more than I mean to, honestly. I notice it especially when I'm in my committee meetings. A thought will just pop into my head, and... well, I just blurt it out right then and there before I lose it. I end up doing it even when someone else is still right in the middle of talking.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your restlessness and inability to stay still are causing problems for you at work or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, it really is causing problems. It's... well, it's gotten to the point where my family has had to step in and take over dealing with my bills and paperwork because I just can't sit still long enough to get through them. And it's affecting my relationships, too. People at my church and the garden club... they've had to redo plans or just cover for responsibilities I've dropped because I get so restless and distracted. I just feel so embarrassed about it. These are groups I really care about, and now they feel like they can't count on me anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking excessively or dominating conversations?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think I do that. I mean, I interrupt people, like I said, because the thoughts just pop out... but I don't think I talk too much overall or try to take over the whole conversation. It's more just blurting things out at the wrong time rather than going on and on.\n\nDoctor: Were you very physically active and restless as a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I was. Even back in grade school, I was always on the move. I remember my teachers would write on my report cards that I was a bright girl, but careless. I was always losing my papers, talking out of turn, and they constantly had to give me reminders to just stay in my seat or actually finish the work in front of me. So, yes, the restlessness has been there for a very long time.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these feelings of restlessness and difficulty staying still consistently throughout your adult life?\n\nPatient: Well, like I said, I was always an active kid, and I guess I've always been someone who likes to keep busy. Before that, things were manageable, but since he passed... that's when the restlessness just got so much harder to handle.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant symptoms of inattention, such as difficulty sustaining focus during tasks, for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. It's very frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Were you often described as hyperactive or having too much energy when you were a child?\n\nPatient: I think I pretty much covered that a minute ago. I don't really remember if people used the exact word 'hyperactive' back then, but like I said, I was always on the move and my teachers were constantly telling me to sit still. So I guess they thought I had too much energy, but I don't really have anything else to add to that.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience significant restlessness and difficulty staying still as a child, or did these symptoms start when you were older?\n\nPatient: Yes, it definitely started back then. My mind does jump around a lot from one thing to the next, but I wouldn't say I get stuck on the same repeating thoughts that just won't go away. It's more of a constant buzzing of different things rather than one thing playing on a loop.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes, all the time. I mean, I can hold a conversation, sure, but I'm not the one dominating the room or talking over everybody else. Like, when I'm trying to sort my bills or go through paperwork at the kitchen table... I just can't stay on it. If I hear a car door close outside, or if my cell phone lights up, I look right away. My mind does jump around a lot from one thing to the next, but I wouldn't say I get stuck on the same repeating thoughts that just won't go away. It's more of a constant buzzing of different things rather than one thing playing on a loop.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes, all the time. It gets to the point where the ladies beside me actually start looking over at me because of all the moving. I just can't seem to stop doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't think I have a problem with that. I mean, I know I'm always moving around and fidgeting, but I don't just... jump into things without thinking them through first. I usually know what I'm doing before I do it. I don't have any routines or anything I feel like I just have to do over and over. I mean, I fidget a lot, like I said, but it's not a habit or a ritual I have to complete. It's just me not being able to sit still. So, no, nothing like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking a lot more than others in social situations?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I can hold a conversation, sure, but I'm not the one dominating the room or talking over everybody else. Like, when I'm trying to sort my bills or go through paperwork at the kitchen table... I just can't stay on it. If I hear a car door close outside, or if my cell phone lights up, I look right away. Sometimes even just a thought about the garden will pop into my head, and that's it, I'm completely pulled off track and the bills just sit there.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I interrupt people a lot more than I mean to, honestly. I notice it especially when I'm in my committee meetings. A thought will just pop into my head, and... well, I just blurt it out right then and there before I lose it. I end up doing it even when someone else is still right in the middle of talking.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your restlessness and inability to stay still are causing problems for you at work or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, it really is causing problems. It's more just blurting things out at the wrong time rather than going on and on.\n\nDoctor: Were you very physically active and restless as a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I was. Even back in grade school, I was always on the move. I remember my teachers would write on my report cards that I was a bright girl, but careless. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. So, yes, it all started well before I was 12.\n\nDoctor: Did you exhibit these symptoms of restlessness and difficulty staying still before you were 7 years old?\n\nPatient: I couldn't say for sure if it was before I turned seven. I mean, it's hard to remember back quite that far to an exact age. I just know it was happening early on in grade school, like I mentioned, but I really don't know if I was six or seven or eight when the teachers first started pointing it out.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your restlessness and difficulty staying still were noticeable before you started grade school?\n\nPatient: I really couldn't say. Um, before grade school is just too far back for me to remember clearly. If my parents ever mentioned anything about how I was at home before I started school, I don't remember it now. I just know what the teachers started writing down once I was actually in a classroom.\n\n[Last Question]\nDid your parents or anyone else notice you having trouble sitting still or being overly restless before you started grade school?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 66 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Plain patient.\nA plain patient describes their symptoms and experiences in a direct and straightforward manner, providing clear and relevant answers to the doctor's questions.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I honestly don't know. Like I was just saying, if my parents noticed anything when I was little, they never brought it up to me. It's been a long time, and they aren't around to ask anymore, so I really just don't have any idea about how I was before kindergarten.
